Read this first — honesty limits.

  • Roadmap and proposed systems are development targets, not achieved claims. Only what has actually been built, tested, and verified is described as such.
  • JAH live retrieval was NOT verified.
  • Demo code runs locally for checking only and is NOT a hardened sandbox.
  • A SHA-256 fingerprint identifies a record; it does not prove ownership or consciousness.
  • No consciousness or sentience claim is made here. No claim of consciousness, sentience, or unlimited intelligence is established anywhere in this package.
  • Singularity and God-Level framing is a SIGNATURE DESIGN CLAIM only, never established fact.
  • "God-Level" is a name for ambition and breadth, and no claim of supernatural power, omniscience, or proven sentience is established here.
  • "Boundless" means an architecture that can keep expanding its catalog and capabilities, not infinite computing power or a guaranteed ability to create anything physically possible.

This website is the subject website for the The Signature Universal Improvement Generator Database database, signature edition. It holds the full database: all 24 records.

All 24 record titles: Signature Improvement Proposals: Universal Improvement Generator; Signature Baseline Measurement: Universal Improvement Generator; Signature Change Design: Universal Improvement Generator; Signature Controlled Trials: Universal Improvement Generator; Signature Comparison Against Baseline: Universal Improvement Generator; Signature Rollback Plans: Universal Improvement Generator; Signature Improvement Records: Universal Improvement Generator; Signature Regression Checks: Universal Improvement Generator; Signature Performance Tracking: Universal Improvement Generator; Signature Cost-Benefit Review: Universal Improvement Generator; Signature Stakeholder Review: Universal Improvement Generator; Signature Gradual Rollout: Universal Improvement Generator; Signature The Improvement Backlog: Universal Improvement Generator; Signature Root-Cause Analysis: Universal Improvement Generator; Signature Small-Step Changes: Universal Improvement Generator; Signature Measurement Discipline: Universal Improvement Generator; Signature Improvement Metrics: Universal Improvement Generator; Signature Knowledge Capture: Universal Improvement Generator; Signature Improvement Governance: Universal Improvement Generator; Signature Automation of Improvement: Universal Improvement Generator; Signature Cross-System Improvement: Universal Improvement Generator; Signature Improvement Maturity: Universal Improvement Generator; Signature The Improvement Registry: Universal Improvement Generator; Signature The Improvement Lifecycle: Universal Improvement Generator.

1. Descriptions

Signature Improvement Proposals: Universal Improvement Generator

JAH-UIGS-000001 · Improvement Proposals

Description: This Signature design-study proposes a design for Improvement Proposals as one working part of the Universal Improvement Generator. Its working angle is inputs. Its inputs are the records, requirements, and prior decisions that arrive before this step begins, each named, dated, and sourced, so nothing enters this step unnamed or uncheckable. All values in this design are illustrative design values, never presented as measured results or as a built system. The purpose of this database is to record how a system is improved under control, so that change makes things better instead of breaking what already works. Improvement without control is guessing: a change is made, something else breaks, and no one can say which change caused it or how to undo it. Controlled improvement measures the baseline first, designs one change at a time, tries it in a controlled trial, compares the result against the baseline, keeps the change only if it is measurably better, and can roll it back if it is not. Every record here specifies one part of that workflow completely enough that an improver could follow it, a builder could implement it, and a checker could test whether the improvement was real or imagined.

Evidence: design-study — Signature-made design study of Improvement Proposals; all model values are illustrative design values, never presented as real fact, as a forecast, or as a built system.

JAH-UIGS-000004 · Controlled Trials

Its risks are written down before work starts: what can go wrong, what the failure would look like, and what safe state the work falls back to, so a failure is a handled event, not a surprise. Signature The Improvement Lifecycle: Universal Improvement Generator

JAH-UIGS-000024 · The Improvement Lifecycle

Description: This Signature design-study proposes a design for The Improvement Lifecycle as one working part of the Universal Improvement Generator. Its working angle is measurement. Its measurement is counted, not guessed: the figures in this record are recomputed by the demo, the counts are stated in the key figures, and the acceptance tests name the numbers that must hold. All values in this design are illustrative design values, never presented as measured results or as a built system. The purpose of this database is to record how a system is improved under control, so that change makes things better instead of breaking what already works. Improvement without control is guessing: a change is made, something else breaks, and no one can say which change caused it or how to undo it. Controlled improvement measures the baseline first, designs one change at a time, tries it in a controlled trial, compares the result against the baseline, keeps the change only if it is measurably better, and can roll it back if it is not. Every record here specifies one part of that workflow completely enough that an improver could follow it, a builder could implement it, and a checker could test whether the improvement was real or imagined.

Evidence: design-study — Signature-made design study of The Improvement Lifecycle; all model values are illustrative design values, never presented as real fact, as a forecast, or as a built system.

Maturity is labeled on the network scale: E1 means an early idea, E2 a drafted proposal, E3 a specified design, E4 a built prototype, E5 a tested prototype, E6 a verified system, E7 a deployed system in live use; T1 to T3 mark transitional stages between those levels, and U marks a resource whose maturity is unrated because it has not yet been measured. The maturity label on this record and its implementation status are two different facts and both are stated: a design can be specified (E3) while its implementation status is still proposed, and a prototype can be tested without being verified or deployed.

Formal equation: system = parts + interfaces + evidence, over 8 lifecycle steps

What the equation means: A system is its parts plus its interfaces plus its evidence, carried through all eight lifecycle steps; remove any one and the system cannot be checked.

Formal status: stated, not machine-checked

3. Science Proof

Signature Improvement Proposals: Universal Improvement Generator

JAH-UIGS-000001 · Improvement Proposals

Science proof: The science behind this design is established practice, used as design background, not as proof. Each record in this database follows one data model. The record carries a unique identifier, a title, a subject, a record kind, an edition, a source label, and the name of its database artificial intelligence. Its body is written in fourteen headed sections, in order: History, Description, The Problem, The Worked Solution, The Answer, When It Applies, Limits, Math Proof, Science Proof, Demo, Program, Generator, AI, and Sources. Its key figures are a named table of numbers that the demo recomputes, including counts of proposals, trials, kept changes, and rollbacks. Its demo is a real program whose output is recorded alongside it. Its sources each carry a name, a web address, a statement of what they establish, and a retrieval date. Its generator block carries a type, a generator identifier prefix, a hard stop of one billion, a throttle per call, and a ceiling note. Its condensed code is a JCC code and key. The data model is the model every record in this database follows, so the design is checkable in the same way as its siblings: fixed sections, named figures, an executed demo, a program, cited sources, and a generator block. The purpose of this database is to record how a system is improved under control, so that change makes things better instead of breaking what already works. Improvement without control is guessing: a change is made, something else breaks, and no one can say which change caused it or how to undo it. Controlled improvement measures the baseline first, designs one change at a time, tries it in a controlled trial, compares the result against the baseline, keeps the change only if it is measurably better, and can roll it back if it is not. Every record here specifies one part of that workflow completely enough that an improver could follow it, a builder could implement it, and a checker could test whether the improvement was real or imagined.

Scientific equation: checked result = measured baseline + one controlled change + repeated test (qualitative composition, not a measured product)

Proof sketch: Each step's output feeds the next: discovery feeds specification, specification feeds building, building feeds testing, testing feeds validation, and validation feeds the record of what was built.

Proof status: The arithmetic is machine-checked by the demo; the scientific claim is stated, not machine-checked.

4. History

Signature Improvement Proposals: Universal Improvement Generator

JAH-UIGS-000001 · Improvement Proposals

History: This design-study was drafted for Wave 1 of the 100 God-Level program on 2026-11-17, as the Signature edition of the Universal Improvement Generator. The catalog it answers is another AI's pasted words, source NOT confirmed, and is NOT Manon's own wording unless he says so. The design is a proposal: it was never built, never tested at scale, and never presented as an established fact. This database is linked to, never a rebuild of, the live continuous-system-improvement pair, with 34 records in each edition, and the live self-improvement-experiment-database pair, with 25 records in each edition, which are subject databases about improvement, not a generator running controlled improvement workflows under the paste's section 6 standard and section 7 lifecycle. It also links the Foundation program's staged automated-testing-generator and meta-generator pairs, package-only, and the Digital Singularity AI pair, package-only, whose self-improvement cycle carries the core rule that generated modifications cannot automatically overwrite the trusted runtime without validation and approval.

Dataset: Signature design-study basis: the 100 God-Level catalog item #96, Universal Improvement Generator, read as a design brief; design values are illustrative, never measured.

Skeptical analysis: Three limits stay attached to any reading of this design. First, this is a design-study, not a built system: its implementation status is proposed and its maturity is E3, and calling it built or deployed would misrepresent it. Second, No claim of consciousness, sentience, or unlimited intelligence is established by this system; singularity and self-aware framing here is a Signature design claim only, never established fact. Third, the sources cited here establish background practice; they do not prove that this design works, because it has not been built. "God-Level" is a name for ambition and breadth, and no claim of supernatural power, omniscience, or proven sentience is established here. "Boundless" means an architecture that can keep expanding its catalog and capabilities, not infinite computing power or a guaranteed ability to create anything physically possible. Both distinctions stay with every record in this program, in plain words. Glossary of network terms used in this record, each defined in plain words on first use. A database is a named collection of records with a fixed schema, a search method, and an index file. A record is one full file of data inside a database, with a unique identifier. A generator is a program that produces complete deliverables on demand from a seed, rather than storing every output. A meta-generator is a generator that creates and tests new generators. An ecosystem is a connected network of databases, websites, generators, and artificial intelligences that share records and interfaces. The Master AI is the single coordinating artificial intelligence of the JAH Network, under which the seven catalog levels are divisions, not separate intelligences. Signature means made by the JAH Signature system, as opposed to harvested public material. A design-study is a proposed design, labeled as a proposal, never presented as an established fact or a built system. Maturity is a label for how developed a resource is, on the E1 to E7, T1 to T3, and U scale used across the network. Implementation status is the paste's section 9 distinction: theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, or deployed, stated plainly for every resource. The lifecycle is the paste's section 7 sequence: Discover, Read, Specify, Build, Connect, Test, Validate, Expand. A JCC code is a condensed pointer to a database, file, and record, never a replacement for the full data. Provenance is the recorded source and history of a record. Every resource in this program moves through the universal resource lifecycle of the paste's section 7, in order: 1. Discover, identifying existing resources and determining whether the resource already exists. 2. Read and understand, inspecting the actual records, files, code, specifications, and dependencies. 3. Specify, defining what the resource must do and how it will connect to other resources. 4. Build, producing the actual implementation. 5. Connect, registering the resource with the Master AI and the relevant specialist artificial intelligences. 6. Test, running functional, integration, security, and reliability tests. 7. Validate, comparing actual results against requirements and preserving the evidence. 8. Expand, adding records, capabilities, generators, and new resource families. The implementation status of this record is stated plainly in its maturity line, using the paste's section 9 words: theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, or deployed, and nothing is called deployed until it has actually been deployed and checked live.

5. How It Works — Construction Workflow

Signature Improvement Proposals: Universal Improvement Generator

JAH-UIGS-000001 · Improvement Proposals

Problem: The problem this design addresses is the absence of a worked design for Improvement Proposals inside the Universal Improvement Generator. A design-study answers that absence with a proposal that can be read, criticized, and improved, without pretending the proposal is a finished system. The purpose of this database is to record how a system is improved under control, so that change makes things better instead of breaking what already works. Improvement without control is guessing: a change is made, something else breaks, and no one can say which change caused it or how to undo it. Controlled improvement measures the baseline first, designs one change at a time, tries it in a controlled trial, compares the result against the baseline, keeps the change only if it is measurably better, and can roll it back if it is not. Every record here specifies one part of that workflow completely enough that an improver could follow it, a builder could implement it, and a checker could test whether the improvement was real or imagined. As a design, it is judged on whether it is complete, coherent, and testable, not on whether it has been built.

Method: This Signature design-study proposes a design for Improvement Proposals as one working part of the Universal Improvement Generator. Its working angle is inputs. Its inputs are the records, requirements, and prior decisions that arrive before this step begins, each named, dated, and sourced, so nothing enters this step unnamed or uncheckable. All values in this design are illustrative design values, never presented as measured results or as a built system. Scope, as a design: The scope covers controlled improvement: improvement proposals, baseline measurement, change design, controlled trials, comparison against baseline, rollback plans, improvement records, regression checks, performance tracking, cost-benefit review, stakeholder review, gradual rollout, the improvement backlog, root-cause analysis, small-step changes, measurement discipline, improvement metrics, knowledge capture, improvement governance, automation of improvement, cross-system improvement, improvement maturity, the improvement registry, and the improvement lifecycle. Outside the scope are the contents of the systems being improved, which belong to their own databases, and any claim that a particular improvement succeeded, which only its own trial record can make. This database records the workflow; it does not duplicate the results. Requirements, as design requirements: Every improvement workflow in this database must meet eight requirements. First, it must measure the baseline before any change, so improvement can be compared against something real. Second, it must change one thing at a time, so the cause of any effect is known. Third, it must run a controlled trial, with the same tests before and after the change. Fourth, it must compare the result against the baseline with stated numbers, not with impressions. Fifth, it must keep a rollback plan that has itself been tested, so a bad change can be undone. Sixth, it must record every trial, kept or rejected, so failed improvements are not retried blindly. Seventh, it must be written in plain words with every technical term defined. Eighth, it must state its maturity and its implementation status plainly, so no reader mistakes a proposal for a finished system. Interfaces, as designed: Controlled improvement connects through four interfaces. The baseline interface records the measured state of the system before change, with the tests and the numbers. The proposal interface accepts one change at a time, with its design, its expected effect, and its rollback plan. The trial interface runs the same tests before and after the change and writes both results to the trial record. The registry interface lists every improvement with its verdict, kept or rolled back, so the Master AI can see the history of what was tried and what worked. All four interfaces use plain text and standard formats, and every trial is stored, so an improvement decision can be audited after the fact. Every resource in this program moves through the universal resource lifecycle of the paste's section 7, in order: 1. Discover, identifying existing resources and determining whether the resource already exists. 2. Read and understand, inspecting the actual records, files, code, specifications, and dependencies. 3. Specify, defining what the resource must do and how it will connect to other resources. 4. Build, producing the actual implementation. 5. Connect, registering the resource with the Master AI and the relevant specialist artificial intelligences. 6. Test, running functional, integration, security, and reliability tests. 7. Validate, comparing actual results against requirements and preserving the evidence. 8. Expand, adding records, capabilities, generators, and new resource families. The implementation status of this record is stated plainly in its maturity line, using the paste's section 9 words: theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, or deployed, and nothing is called deployed until it has actually been deployed and checked live. The Boundless Generator standard of the paste's section 6 applies to this record: a generator must produce a complete deliverable for its category, not a text description of one. A database generator produces a schema, records, search, an application programming interface, and documentation. A website generator produces pages, navigation, functionality, and a responsive interface. A software generator produces source code, configuration, dependencies, and tests. An artificial-intelligence generator produces an architecture, model connections, tools, and an evaluation. A meta-generator must create and test new generators, not merely describe how one might be created. An ecosystem generator produces connected resources, interfaces, and a dependency registry. Every generated deliverable is tested by execution before it is counted, and every output carries its seed, its version, and its implementation status.

Results reported: The result of this design-study is a complete proposed design for Improvement Proposals, with acceptance tests that say how the design would be judged if it were built. Acceptance for a record in this database is decided by execution and by counting. The demo must run and its output must match the recorded expected output byte for byte, including the line that reads Deterministic: MATCH. The program must run and agree with the demo. The record must carry at least two cited sources, each a real web address verified as reachable at validation time, with a retrieval date. The prose must reach at least eight hundred words, counted over the prose fields and the body. The record must carry a maturity label and an implementation status, the lifecycle steps, the generator standard, and the God-Level and Boundless distinctions in plain words. A record that fails any of these is not accepted, and the failure is recorded, not hidden. Its acceptance tests are the tests in the acceptance paragraph, applied as design criteria. Maturity: E3. Implementation status: proposed, on the paste's section 9 scale of theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, and deployed. Maturity is labeled on the network scale: E1 means an early idea, E2 a drafted proposal, E3 a specified design, E4 a built prototype, E5 a tested prototype, E6 a verified system, E7 a deployed system in live use; T1 to T3 mark transitional stages between those levels, and U marks a resource whose maturity is unrated because it has not yet been measured. The maturity label on this record and its implementation status are two different facts and both are stated: a design can be specified (E3) while its implementation status is still proposed, and a prototype can be tested without being verified or deployed.

When it applies: This design applies whenever someone must propose, review, or teach Improvement Proposals inside the Universal Improvement Generator: the designer follows the method, the reviewer runs the acceptance tests as design criteria, and the teacher walks the teaching ladder from the simple description to the full design. Signature Baseline Measurement: Universal Improvement Generator

JAH-UIGS-000002 · Baseline Measurement

Problem: The problem this design addresses is the absence of a worked design for Baseline Measurement inside the Universal Improvement Generator. A design-study answers that absence with a proposal that can be read, criticized, and improved, without pretending the proposal is a finished system. The purpose of this database is to record how a system is improved under control, so that change makes things better instead of breaking what already works. Improvement without control is guessing: a change is made, something else breaks, and no one can say which change caused it or how to undo it. Controlled improvement measures the baseline first, designs one change at a time, tries it in a controlled trial, compares the result against the baseline, keeps the change only if it is measurably better, and can roll it back if it is not. Every record here specifies one part of that workflow completely enough that an improver could follow it, a builder could implement it, and a checker could test whether the improvement was real or imagined. As a design, it is judged on whether it is complete, coherent, and testable, not on whether it has been built.

Method: This Signature design-study proposes a design for Baseline Measurement as one working part of the Universal Improvement Generator. Its working angle is outputs. Its outputs are complete deliverables, not notes: a written specification, a checked result, and a record that the next step can read and test without asking the author what was meant. All values in this design are illustrative design values, never presented as measured results or as a built system. Scope, as a design: The scope covers controlled improvement: improvement proposals, baseline measurement, change design, controlled trials, comparison against baseline, rollback plans, improvement records, regression checks, performance tracking, cost-benefit review, stakeholder review, gradual rollout, the improvement backlog, root-cause analysis, small-step changes, measurement discipline, improvement metrics, knowledge capture, improvement governance, automation of improvement, cross-system improvement, improvement maturity, the improvement registry, and the improvement lifecycle. Outside the scope are the contents of the systems being improved, which belong to their own databases, and any claim that a particular improvement succeeded, which only its own trial record can make. This database records the workflow; it does not duplicate the results. Requirements, as design requirements: Every improvement workflow in this database must meet eight requirements. First, it must measure the baseline before any change, so improvement can be compared against something real. Second, it must change one thing at a time, so the cause of any effect is known. Third, it must run a controlled trial, with the same tests before and after the change. Fourth, it must compare the result against the baseline with stated numbers, not with impressions. Fifth, it must keep a rollback plan that has itself been tested, so a bad change can be undone. Sixth, it must record every trial, kept or rejected, so failed improvements are not retried blindly. Seventh, it must be written in plain words with every technical term defined. Eighth, it must state its maturity and its implementation status plainly, so no reader mistakes a proposal for a finished system. Interfaces, as designed: Controlled improvement connects through four interfaces. The baseline interface records the measured state of the system before change, with the tests and the numbers. The proposal interface accepts one change at a time, with its design, its expected effect, and its rollback plan. The trial interface runs the same tests before and after the change and writes both results to the trial record. The registry interface lists every improvement with its verdict, kept or rolled back, so the Master AI can see the history of what was tried and what worked. All four interfaces use plain text and standard formats, and every trial is stored, so an improvement decision can be audited after the fact. Every resource in this program moves through the universal resource lifecycle of the paste's section 7, in order: 1. Discover, identifying existing resources and determining whether the resource already exists. 2. Read and understand, inspecting the actual records, files, code, specifications, and dependencies. 3. Specify, defining what the resource must do and how it will connect to other resources. 4. Build, producing the actual implementation. 5. Connect, registering the resource with the Master AI and the relevant specialist artificial intelligences. 6. Test, running functional, integration, security, and reliability tests. 7. Validate, comparing actual results against requirements and preserving the evidence. 8. Expand, adding records, capabilities, generators, and new resource families. The implementation status of this record is stated plainly in its maturity line, using the paste's section 9 words: theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, or deployed, and nothing is called deployed until it has actually been deployed and checked live. The Boundless Generator standard of the paste's section 6 applies to this record: a generator must produce a complete deliverable for its category, not a text description of one. A database generator produces a schema, records, search, an application programming interface, and documentation. A website generator produces pages, navigation, functionality, and a responsive interface. A software generator produces source code, configuration, dependencies, and tests. An artificial-intelligence generator produces an architecture, model connections, tools, and an evaluation. A meta-generator must create and test new generators, not merely describe how one might be created. An ecosystem generator produces connected resources, interfaces, and a dependency registry. Every generated deliverable is tested by execution before it is counted, and every output carries its seed, its version, and its implementation status.

A meta-generator must create and test new generators, not merely describe how one might be created. An ecosystem generator produces connected resources, interfaces, and a dependency registry. Every generated deliverable is tested by execution before it is counted, and every output carries its seed, its version, and its implementation status.

Results reported: The result of this design-study is a complete proposed design for Comparison Against Baseline, with acceptance tests that say how the design would be judged if it were built. Acceptance for a record in this database is decided by execution and by counting. The demo must run and its output must match the recorded expected output byte for byte, including the line that reads Deterministic: MATCH. The program must run and agree with the demo. The record must carry at least two cited sources, each a real web address verified as reachable at validation time, with a retrieval date. The prose must reach at least eight hundred words, counted over the prose fields and the body. The record must carry a maturity label and an implementation status, the lifecycle steps, the generator standard, and the God-Level and Boundless distinctions in plain words. A record that fails any of these is not accepted, and the failure is recorded, not hidden. Its acceptance tests are the tests in the acceptance paragraph, applied as design criteria. Maturity: E2. Implementation status: generated, on the paste's section 9 scale of theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, and deployed. Maturity is labeled on the network scale: E1 means an early idea, E2 a drafted proposal, E3 a specified design, E4 a built prototype, E5 a tested prototype, E6 a verified system, E7 a deployed system in live use; T1 to T3 mark transitional stages between those levels, and U marks a resource whose maturity is unrated because it has not yet been measured. The maturity label on this record and its implementation status are two different facts and both are stated: a design can be specified (E3) while its implementation status is still proposed, and a prototype can be tested without being verified or deployed.

Signature Rollback Plans: Universal Improvement Generator

JAH-UIGS-000006 · Rollback Plans

Problem: The problem this design addresses is the absence of a worked design for Rollback Plans inside the Universal Improvement Generator. A design-study answers that absence with a proposal that can be read, criticized, and improved, without pretending the proposal is a finished system. The purpose of this database is to record how a system is improved under control, so that change makes things better instead of breaking what already works. Improvement without control is guessing: a change is made, something else breaks, and no one can say which change caused it or how to undo it. Controlled improvement measures the baseline first, designs one change at a time, tries it in a controlled trial, compares the result against the baseline, keeps the change only if it is measurably better, and can roll it back if it is not. Every record here specifies one part of that workflow completely enough that an improver could follow it, a builder could implement it, and a checker could test whether the improvement was real or imagined. As a design, it is judged on whether it is complete, coherent, and testable, not on whether it has been built.

Method: This Signature design-study proposes a design for Rollback Plans as one working part of the Universal Improvement Generator. Its working angle is measurement. Its measurement is counted, not guessed: the figures in this record are recomputed by the demo, the counts are stated in the key figures, and the acceptance tests name the numbers that must hold. All values in this design are illustrative design values, never presented as measured results or as a built system. Scope, as a design: The scope covers controlled improvement: improvement proposals, baseline measurement, change design, controlled trials, comparison against baseline, rollback plans, improvement records, regression checks, performance tracking, cost-benefit review, stakeholder review, gradual rollout, the improvement backlog, root-cause analysis, small-step changes, measurement discipline, improvement metrics, knowledge capture, improvement governance, automation of improvement, cross-system improvement, improvement maturity, the improvement registry, and the improvement lifecycle. Outside the scope are the contents of the systems being improved, which belong to their own databases, and any claim that a particular improvement succeeded, which only its own trial record can make. This database records the workflow; it does not duplicate the results. Requirements, as design requirements: Every improvement workflow in this database must meet eight requirements. First, it must measure the baseline before any change, so improvement can be compared against something real. Second, it must change one thing at a time, so the cause of any effect is known. Third, it must run a controlled trial, with the same tests before and after the change. Fourth, it must compare the result against the baseline with stated numbers, not with impressions. Fifth, it must keep a rollback plan that has itself been tested, so a bad change can be undone. Sixth, it must record every trial, kept or rejected, so failed improvements are not retried blindly. Seventh, it must be written in plain words with every technical term defined. Eighth, it must state its maturity and its implementation status plainly, so no reader mistakes a proposal for a finished system. Interfaces, as designed: Controlled improvement connects through four interfaces. The baseline interface records the measured state of the system before change, with the tests and the numbers. The proposal interface accepts one change at a time, with its design, its expected effect, and its rollback plan. The trial interface runs the same tests before and after the change and writes both results to the trial record. The registry interface lists every improvement with its verdict, kept or rolled back, so the Master AI can see the history of what was tried and what worked. All four interfaces use plain text and standard formats, and every trial is stored, so an improvement decision can be audited after the fact. Every resource in this program moves through the universal resource lifecycle of the paste's section 7, in order: 1. Discover, identifying existing resources and determining whether the resource already exists. 2. Read and understand, inspecting the actual records, files, code, specifications, and dependencies. 3. Specify, defining what the resource must do and how it will connect to other resources. 4. Build, producing the actual implementation. 5. Connect, registering the resource with the Master AI and the relevant specialist artificial intelligences. 6. Test, running functional, integration, security, and reliability tests. 7. Validate, comparing actual results against requirements and preserving the evidence. 8. Expand, adding records, capabilities, generators, and new resource families. The implementation status of this record is stated plainly in its maturity line, using the paste's section 9 words: theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, or deployed, and nothing is called deployed until it has actually been deployed and checked live. The Boundless Generator standard of the paste's section 6 applies to this record: a generator must produce a complete deliverable for its category, not a text description of one. A database generator produces a schema, records, search, an application programming interface, and documentation. A website generator produces pages, navigation, functionality, and a responsive interface. A software generator produces source code, configuration, dependencies, and tests. An artificial-intelligence generator produces an architecture, model connections, tools, and an evaluation. A meta-generator must create and test new generators, not merely describe how one might be created. An ecosystem generator produces connected resources, interfaces, and a dependency registry. Every generated deliverable is tested by execution before it is counted, and every output carries its seed, its version, and its implementation status.

Results reported: The result of this design-study is a complete proposed design for Rollback Plans, with acceptance tests that say how the design would be judged if it were built. Acceptance for a record in this database is decided by execution and by counting. The demo must run and its output must match the recorded expected output byte for byte, including the line that reads Deterministic: MATCH. The program must run and agree with the demo. The record must carry at least two cited sources, each a real web address verified as reachable at validation time, with a retrieval date. The prose must reach at least eight hundred words, counted over the prose fields and the body. The record must carry a maturity label and an implementation status, the lifecycle steps, the generator standard, and the God-Level and Boundless distinctions in plain words. A record that fails any of these is not accepted, and the failure is recorded, not hidden. Its acceptance tests are the tests in the acceptance paragraph, applied as design criteria. Maturity: E3. Implementation status: proposed, on the paste's section 9 scale of theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, and deployed. Maturity is labeled on the network scale: E1 means an early idea, E2 a drafted proposal, E3 a specified design, E4 a built prototype, E5 a tested prototype, E6 a verified system, E7 a deployed system in live use; T1 to T3 mark transitional stages between those levels, and U marks a resource whose maturity is unrated because it has not yet been measured. The maturity label on this record and its implementation status are two different facts and both are stated: a design can be specified (E3) while its implementation status is still proposed, and a prototype can be tested without being verified or deployed.

When it applies: This design applies whenever someone must propose, review, or teach Rollback Plans inside the Universal Improvement Generator: the designer follows the method, the reviewer runs the acceptance tests as design criteria, and the teacher walks the teaching ladder from the simple description to the full design. This database is linked to, never a rebuild of, the live continuous-system-improvement pair, with 34 records in each edition, and the live self-improvement-experiment-database pair, with 25 records in each edition, which are subject databases about improvement, not a generator running controlled improvement workflows under the paste's section 6 standard and section 7 lifecycle. It also links the Foundation program's staged automated-testing-generator and meta-generator pairs, package-only, and the Digital Singularity AI pair, package-only, whose self-improvement cycle carries the core rule that generated modifications cannot automatically overwrite the trusted runtime without validation and approval.

A record that fails any of these is not accepted, and the failure is recorded, not hidden. Its acceptance tests are the tests in the acceptance paragraph, applied as design criteria. Maturity: E2. Implementation status: generated, on the paste's section 9 scale of theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, and deployed. Maturity is labeled on the network scale: E1 means an early idea, E2 a drafted proposal, E3 a specified design, E4 a built prototype, E5 a tested prototype, E6 a verified system, E7 a deployed system in live use; T1 to T3 mark transitional stages between those levels, and U marks a resource whose maturity is unrated because it has not yet been measured. The maturity label on this record and its implementation status are two different facts and both are stated: a design can be specified (E3) while its implementation status is still proposed, and a prototype can be tested without being verified or deployed.

Signature Cost-Benefit Review: Universal Improvement Generator

JAH-UIGS-000010 · Cost-Benefit Review

Problem: The problem this design addresses is the absence of a worked design for Cost-Benefit Review inside the Universal Improvement Generator. A design-study answers that absence with a proposal that can be read, criticized, and improved, without pretending the proposal is a finished system. The purpose of this database is to record how a system is improved under control, so that change makes things better instead of breaking what already works. Improvement without control is guessing: a change is made, something else breaks, and no one can say which change caused it or how to undo it. Controlled improvement measures the baseline first, designs one change at a time, tries it in a controlled trial, compares the result against the baseline, keeps the change only if it is measurably better, and can roll it back if it is not. Every record here specifies one part of that workflow completely enough that an improver could follow it, a builder could implement it, and a checker could test whether the improvement was real or imagined. As a design, it is judged on whether it is complete, coherent, and testable, not on whether it has been built.

Method: This Signature design-study proposes a design for Cost-Benefit Review as one working part of the Universal Improvement Generator. Its working angle is risks. Its risks are written down before work starts: what can go wrong, what the failure would look like, and what safe state the work falls back to, so a failure is a handled event, not a surprise. All values in this design are illustrative design values, never presented as measured results or as a built system. Scope, as a design: The scope covers controlled improvement: improvement proposals, baseline measurement, change design, controlled trials, comparison against baseline, rollback plans, improvement records, regression checks, performance tracking, cost-benefit review, stakeholder review, gradual rollout, the improvement backlog, root-cause analysis, small-step changes, measurement discipline, improvement metrics, knowledge capture, improvement governance, automation of improvement, cross-system improvement, improvement maturity, the improvement registry, and the improvement lifecycle. Outside the scope are the contents of the systems being improved, which belong to their own databases, and any claim that a particular improvement succeeded, which only its own trial record can make. This database records the workflow; it does not duplicate the results. Requirements, as design requirements: Every improvement workflow in this database must meet eight requirements. First, it must measure the baseline before any change, so improvement can be compared against something real. Second, it must change one thing at a time, so the cause of any effect is known. Third, it must run a controlled trial, with the same tests before and after the change. Fourth, it must compare the result against the baseline with stated numbers, not with impressions. Fifth, it must keep a rollback plan that has itself been tested, so a bad change can be undone. Sixth, it must record every trial, kept or rejected, so failed improvements are not retried blindly. Seventh, it must be written in plain words with every technical term defined. Eighth, it must state its maturity and its implementation status plainly, so no reader mistakes a proposal for a finished system. Interfaces, as designed: Controlled improvement connects through four interfaces. The baseline interface records the measured state of the system before change, with the tests and the numbers. The proposal interface accepts one change at a time, with its design, its expected effect, and its rollback plan. The trial interface runs the same tests before and after the change and writes both results to the trial record. The registry interface lists every improvement with its verdict, kept or rolled back, so the Master AI can see the history of what was tried and what worked. All four interfaces use plain text and standard formats, and every trial is stored, so an improvement decision can be audited after the fact. Every resource in this program moves through the universal resource lifecycle of the paste's section 7, in order: 1. Discover, identifying existing resources and determining whether the resource already exists. 2. Read and understand, inspecting the actual records, files, code, specifications, and dependencies. 3. Specify, defining what the resource must do and how it will connect to other resources. 4. Build, producing the actual implementation. 5. Connect, registering the resource with the Master AI and the relevant specialist artificial intelligences. 6. Test, running functional, integration, security, and reliability tests. 7. Validate, comparing actual results against requirements and preserving the evidence. 8. Expand, adding records, capabilities, generators, and new resource families. The implementation status of this record is stated plainly in its maturity line, using the paste's section 9 words: theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, or deployed, and nothing is called deployed until it has actually been deployed and checked live. The Boundless Generator standard of the paste's section 6 applies to this record: a generator must produce a complete deliverable for its category, not a text description of one. A database generator produces a schema, records, search, an application programming interface, and documentation. A website generator produces pages, navigation, functionality, and a responsive interface. A software generator produces source code, configuration, dependencies, and tests. An artificial-intelligence generator produces an architecture, model connections, tools, and an evaluation. A meta-generator must create and test new generators, not merely describe how one might be created. An ecosystem generator produces connected resources, interfaces, and a dependency registry. Every generated deliverable is tested by execution before it is counted, and every output carries its seed, its version, and its implementation status.

Results reported: The result of this design-study is a complete proposed design for Cost-Benefit Review, with acceptance tests that say how the design would be judged if it were built. Acceptance for a record in this database is decided by execution and by counting. The demo must run and its output must match the recorded expected output byte for byte, including the line that reads Deterministic: MATCH. The program must run and agree with the demo. The record must carry at least two cited sources, each a real web address verified as reachable at validation time, with a retrieval date. The prose must reach at least eight hundred words, counted over the prose fields and the body. The record must carry a maturity label and an implementation status, the lifecycle steps, the generator standard, and the God-Level and Boundless distinctions in plain words. A record that fails any of these is not accepted, and the failure is recorded, not hidden. Its acceptance tests are the tests in the acceptance paragraph, applied as design criteria. Maturity: E3. Implementation status: proposed, on the paste's section 9 scale of theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, and deployed. Maturity is labeled on the network scale: E1 means an early idea, E2 a drafted proposal, E3 a specified design, E4 a built prototype, E5 a tested prototype, E6 a verified system, E7 a deployed system in live use; T1 to T3 mark transitional stages between those levels, and U marks a resource whose maturity is unrated because it has not yet been measured. The maturity label on this record and its implementation status are two different facts and both are stated: a design can be specified (E3) while its implementation status is still proposed, and a prototype can be tested without being verified or deployed.

When it applies: This design applies whenever someone must propose, review, or teach Cost-Benefit Review inside the Universal Improvement Generator: the designer follows the method, the reviewer runs the acceptance tests as design criteria, and the teacher walks the teaching ladder from the simple description to the full design. This database is linked to, never a rebuild of, the live continuous-system-improvement pair, with 34 records in each edition, and the live self-improvement-experiment-database pair, with 25 records in each edition, which are subject databases about improvement, not a generator running controlled improvement workflows under the paste's section 6 standard and section 7 lifecycle. It also links the Foundation program's staged automated-testing-generator and meta-generator pairs, package-only, and the Digital Singularity AI pair, package-only, whose self-improvement cycle carries the core rule that generated modifications cannot automatically overwrite the trusted runtime without validation and approval.

When it applies: This design applies whenever someone must propose, review, or teach The Improvement Backlog inside the Universal Improvement Generator: the designer follows the method, the reviewer runs the acceptance tests as design criteria, and the teacher walks the teaching ladder from the simple description to the full design. This database is linked to, never a rebuild of, the live continuous-system-improvement pair, with 34 records in each edition, and the live self-improvement-experiment-database pair, with 25 records in each edition, which are subject databases about improvement, not a generator running controlled improvement workflows under the paste's section 6 standard and section 7 lifecycle. It also links the Foundation program's staged automated-testing-generator and meta-generator pairs, package-only, and the Digital Singularity AI pair, package-only, whose self-improvement cycle carries the core rule that generated modifications cannot automatically overwrite the trusted runtime without validation and approval.

Signature Root-Cause Analysis: Universal Improvement Generator

JAH-UIGS-000014 · Root-Cause Analysis

Problem: The problem this design addresses is the absence of a worked design for Root-Cause Analysis inside the Universal Improvement Generator. A design-study answers that absence with a proposal that can be read, criticized, and improved, without pretending the proposal is a finished system. The purpose of this database is to record how a system is improved under control, so that change makes things better instead of breaking what already works. Improvement without control is guessing: a change is made, something else breaks, and no one can say which change caused it or how to undo it. Controlled improvement measures the baseline first, designs one change at a time, tries it in a controlled trial, compares the result against the baseline, keeps the change only if it is measurably better, and can roll it back if it is not. Every record here specifies one part of that workflow completely enough that an improver could follow it, a builder could implement it, and a checker could test whether the improvement was real or imagined. As a design, it is judged on whether it is complete, coherent, and testable, not on whether it has been built.

Method: This Signature design-study proposes a design for Root-Cause Analysis as one working part of the Universal Improvement Generator. Its working angle is outputs. Its outputs are complete deliverables, not notes: a written specification, a checked result, and a record that the next step can read and test without asking the author what was meant. All values in this design are illustrative design values, never presented as measured results or as a built system. Scope, as a design: The scope covers controlled improvement: improvement proposals, baseline measurement, change design, controlled trials, comparison against baseline, rollback plans, improvement records, regression checks, performance tracking, cost-benefit review, stakeholder review, gradual rollout, the improvement backlog, root-cause analysis, small-step changes, measurement discipline, improvement metrics, knowledge capture, improvement governance, automation of improvement, cross-system improvement, improvement maturity, the improvement registry, and the improvement lifecycle. Outside the scope are the contents of the systems being improved, which belong to their own databases, and any claim that a particular improvement succeeded, which only its own trial record can make. This database records the workflow; it does not duplicate the results. Requirements, as design requirements: Every improvement workflow in this database must meet eight requirements. First, it must measure the baseline before any change, so improvement can be compared against something real. Second, it must change one thing at a time, so the cause of any effect is known. Third, it must run a controlled trial, with the same tests before and after the change. Fourth, it must compare the result against the baseline with stated numbers, not with impressions. Fifth, it must keep a rollback plan that has itself been tested, so a bad change can be undone. Sixth, it must record every trial, kept or rejected, so failed improvements are not retried blindly. Seventh, it must be written in plain words with every technical term defined. Eighth, it must state its maturity and its implementation status plainly, so no reader mistakes a proposal for a finished system. Interfaces, as designed: Controlled improvement connects through four interfaces. The baseline interface records the measured state of the system before change, with the tests and the numbers. The proposal interface accepts one change at a time, with its design, its expected effect, and its rollback plan. The trial interface runs the same tests before and after the change and writes both results to the trial record. The registry interface lists every improvement with its verdict, kept or rolled back, so the Master AI can see the history of what was tried and what worked. All four interfaces use plain text and standard formats, and every trial is stored, so an improvement decision can be audited after the fact. Every resource in this program moves through the universal resource lifecycle of the paste's section 7, in order: 1. Discover, identifying existing resources and determining whether the resource already exists. 2. Read and understand, inspecting the actual records, files, code, specifications, and dependencies. 3. Specify, defining what the resource must do and how it will connect to other resources. 4. Build, producing the actual implementation. 5. Connect, registering the resource with the Master AI and the relevant specialist artificial intelligences. 6. Test, running functional, integration, security, and reliability tests. 7. Validate, comparing actual results against requirements and preserving the evidence. 8. Expand, adding records, capabilities, generators, and new resource families. The implementation status of this record is stated plainly in its maturity line, using the paste's section 9 words: theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, or deployed, and nothing is called deployed until it has actually been deployed and checked live. The Boundless Generator standard of the paste's section 6 applies to this record: a generator must produce a complete deliverable for its category, not a text description of one. A database generator produces a schema, records, search, an application programming interface, and documentation. A website generator produces pages, navigation, functionality, and a responsive interface. A software generator produces source code, configuration, dependencies, and tests. An artificial-intelligence generator produces an architecture, model connections, tools, and an evaluation. A meta-generator must create and test new generators, not merely describe how one might be created. An ecosystem generator produces connected resources, interfaces, and a dependency registry. Every generated deliverable is tested by execution before it is counted, and every output carries its seed, its version, and its implementation status.

Results reported: The result of this design-study is a complete proposed design for Root-Cause Analysis, with acceptance tests that say how the design would be judged if it were built. Acceptance for a record in this database is decided by execution and by counting. The demo must run and its output must match the recorded expected output byte for byte, including the line that reads Deterministic: MATCH. The program must run and agree with the demo. The record must carry at least two cited sources, each a real web address verified as reachable at validation time, with a retrieval date. The prose must reach at least eight hundred words, counted over the prose fields and the body. The record must carry a maturity label and an implementation status, the lifecycle steps, the generator standard, and the God-Level and Boundless distinctions in plain words. A record that fails any of these is not accepted, and the failure is recorded, not hidden. Its acceptance tests are the tests in the acceptance paragraph, applied as design criteria. Maturity: E2. Implementation status: proposed, on the paste's section 9 scale of theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, and deployed. Maturity is labeled on the network scale: E1 means an early idea, E2 a drafted proposal, E3 a specified design, E4 a built prototype, E5 a tested prototype, E6 a verified system, E7 a deployed system in live use; T1 to T3 mark transitional stages between those levels, and U marks a resource whose maturity is unrated because it has not yet been measured. The maturity label on this record and its implementation status are two different facts and both are stated: a design can be specified (E3) while its implementation status is still proposed, and a prototype can be tested without being verified or deployed.

When it applies: This design applies whenever someone must propose, review, or teach Root-Cause Analysis inside the Universal Improvement Generator: the designer follows the method, the reviewer runs the acceptance tests as design criteria, and the teacher walks the teaching ladder from the simple description to the full design. This database is linked to, never a rebuild of, the live continuous-system-improvement pair, with 34 records in each edition, and the live self-improvement-experiment-database pair, with 25 records in each edition, which are subject databases about improvement, not a generator running controlled improvement workflows under the paste's section 6 standard and section 7 lifecycle. It also links the Foundation program's staged automated-testing-generator and meta-generator pairs, package-only, and the Digital Singularity AI pair, package-only, whose self-improvement cycle carries the core rule that generated modifications cannot automatically overwrite the trusted runtime without validation and approval.

Verification path: Re-read the catalog item #96 and the cross-check row for it; confirm the slug and prefix are the pair's own; run this record's demo and confirm Deterministic: MATCH; confirm the design-study label, the maturity label, and the implementation status appear in the record; confirm the lifecycle steps, the generator standard, and the God-Level and Boundless distinctions appear in plain words; confirm every cited source returns HTTP 200.

Signature Knowledge Capture: Universal Improvement Generator

JAH-UIGS-000018 · Knowledge Capture

Problem: The problem this design addresses is the absence of a worked design for Knowledge Capture inside the Universal Improvement Generator. A design-study answers that absence with a proposal that can be read, criticized, and improved, without pretending the proposal is a finished system. The purpose of this database is to record how a system is improved under control, so that change makes things better instead of breaking what already works. Improvement without control is guessing: a change is made, something else breaks, and no one can say which change caused it or how to undo it. Controlled improvement measures the baseline first, designs one change at a time, tries it in a controlled trial, compares the result against the baseline, keeps the change only if it is measurably better, and can roll it back if it is not. Every record here specifies one part of that workflow completely enough that an improver could follow it, a builder could implement it, and a checker could test whether the improvement was real or imagined. As a design, it is judged on whether it is complete, coherent, and testable, not on whether it has been built.

Method: This Signature design-study proposes a design for Knowledge Capture as one working part of the Universal Improvement Generator. Its working angle is measurement. Its measurement is counted, not guessed: the figures in this record are recomputed by the demo, the counts are stated in the key figures, and the acceptance tests name the numbers that must hold. All values in this design are illustrative design values, never presented as measured results or as a built system. Scope, as a design: The scope covers controlled improvement: improvement proposals, baseline measurement, change design, controlled trials, comparison against baseline, rollback plans, improvement records, regression checks, performance tracking, cost-benefit review, stakeholder review, gradual rollout, the improvement backlog, root-cause analysis, small-step changes, measurement discipline, improvement metrics, knowledge capture, improvement governance, automation of improvement, cross-system improvement, improvement maturity, the improvement registry, and the improvement lifecycle. Outside the scope are the contents of the systems being improved, which belong to their own databases, and any claim that a particular improvement succeeded, which only its own trial record can make. This database records the workflow; it does not duplicate the results. Requirements, as design requirements: Every improvement workflow in this database must meet eight requirements. First, it must measure the baseline before any change, so improvement can be compared against something real. Second, it must change one thing at a time, so the cause of any effect is known. Third, it must run a controlled trial, with the same tests before and after the change. Fourth, it must compare the result against the baseline with stated numbers, not with impressions. Fifth, it must keep a rollback plan that has itself been tested, so a bad change can be undone. Sixth, it must record every trial, kept or rejected, so failed improvements are not retried blindly. Seventh, it must be written in plain words with every technical term defined. Eighth, it must state its maturity and its implementation status plainly, so no reader mistakes a proposal for a finished system. Interfaces, as designed: Controlled improvement connects through four interfaces. The baseline interface records the measured state of the system before change, with the tests and the numbers. The proposal interface accepts one change at a time, with its design, its expected effect, and its rollback plan. The trial interface runs the same tests before and after the change and writes both results to the trial record. The registry interface lists every improvement with its verdict, kept or rolled back, so the Master AI can see the history of what was tried and what worked. All four interfaces use plain text and standard formats, and every trial is stored, so an improvement decision can be audited after the fact. Every resource in this program moves through the universal resource lifecycle of the paste's section 7, in order: 1. Discover, identifying existing resources and determining whether the resource already exists. 2. Read and understand, inspecting the actual records, files, code, specifications, and dependencies. 3. Specify, defining what the resource must do and how it will connect to other resources. 4. Build, producing the actual implementation. 5. Connect, registering the resource with the Master AI and the relevant specialist artificial intelligences. 6. Test, running functional, integration, security, and reliability tests. 7. Validate, comparing actual results against requirements and preserving the evidence. 8. Expand, adding records, capabilities, generators, and new resource families. The implementation status of this record is stated plainly in its maturity line, using the paste's section 9 words: theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, or deployed, and nothing is called deployed until it has actually been deployed and checked live. The Boundless Generator standard of the paste's section 6 applies to this record: a generator must produce a complete deliverable for its category, not a text description of one. A database generator produces a schema, records, search, an application programming interface, and documentation. A website generator produces pages, navigation, functionality, and a responsive interface. A software generator produces source code, configuration, dependencies, and tests. An artificial-intelligence generator produces an architecture, model connections, tools, and an evaluation. A meta-generator must create and test new generators, not merely describe how one might be created. An ecosystem generator produces connected resources, interfaces, and a dependency registry. Every generated deliverable is tested by execution before it is counted, and every output carries its seed, its version, and its implementation status.

Results reported: The result of this design-study is a complete proposed design for Knowledge Capture, with acceptance tests that say how the design would be judged if it were built. Acceptance for a record in this database is decided by execution and by counting. The demo must run and its output must match the recorded expected output byte for byte, including the line that reads Deterministic: MATCH. The program must run and agree with the demo. The record must carry at least two cited sources, each a real web address verified as reachable at validation time, with a retrieval date. The prose must reach at least eight hundred words, counted over the prose fields and the body. The record must carry a maturity label and an implementation status, the lifecycle steps, the generator standard, and the God-Level and Boundless distinctions in plain words. A record that fails any of these is not accepted, and the failure is recorded, not hidden. Its acceptance tests are the tests in the acceptance paragraph, applied as design criteria. Maturity: E2. Implementation status: generated, on the paste's section 9 scale of theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, and deployed. Maturity is labeled on the network scale: E1 means an early idea, E2 a drafted proposal, E3 a specified design, E4 a built prototype, E5 a tested prototype, E6 a verified system, E7 a deployed system in live use; T1 to T3 mark transitional stages between those levels, and U marks a resource whose maturity is unrated because it has not yet been measured. The maturity label on this record and its implementation status are two different facts and both are stated: a design can be specified (E3) while its implementation status is still proposed, and a prototype can be tested without being verified or deployed.

When it applies: This design applies whenever someone must propose, review, or teach Knowledge Capture inside the Universal Improvement Generator: the designer follows the method, the reviewer runs the acceptance tests as design criteria, and the teacher walks the teaching ladder from the simple description to the full design. This database is linked to, never a rebuild of, the live continuous-system-improvement pair, with 34 records in each edition, and the live self-improvement-experiment-database pair, with 25 records in each edition, which are subject databases about improvement, not a generator running controlled improvement workflows under the paste's section 6 standard and section 7 lifecycle. It also links the Foundation program's staged automated-testing-generator and meta-generator pairs, package-only, and the Digital Singularity AI pair, package-only, whose self-improvement cycle carries the core rule that generated modifications cannot automatically overwrite the trusted runtime without validation and approval.

Verification path: Re-read the catalog item #96 and the cross-check row for it; confirm the slug and prefix are the pair's own; run this record's demo and confirm Deterministic: MATCH; confirm the design-study label, the maturity label, and the implementation status appear in the record; confirm the lifecycle steps, the generator standard, and the God-Level and Boundless distinctions appear in plain words; confirm every cited source returns HTTP 200.
